13th century CE · Siblings of Dnyaneshwar · Nath-Datta family
📖 Holy Text: Foundational to the Nath-Datta tradition; indirect spiritual ancestors of the Gurucharitra (Book 2)
🔗 How mentioned: Not named in the three canonical texts. Their significance is familial with Dnyaneshwar — all four children of Vitthalpant and Rukmini became saints. Their samadhi shrines at Sasvad (Sopandev) and Alandi (Muktabai) form part of the Maharashtra pilgrimage circuit that Datta devotees traverse alongside Gangapur and Narsobachi Wadi.
⏳ Time gap: Dnyaneshwar's era 1275–1296 CE → Gurucharitra composed circa 1510 CE → texts reference this tradition — ~214 years separates the Dnyaneshwar-Sopandev family from the Gurucharitra's composition
Muktabai's legacy: Tati Ugha Dnyaneshwara (Open the door, Dnyaneshwar) — sung when Dnyaneshwar sat in anger inside a room — is one of the most beautiful and profound poems in Marathi devotional literature. At the age of a child, Muktabai is said to have attained full liberation, her name itself meaning "she who is liberated."
Bal Gangadhar Tilak (Lokmanya)
1856–1920 CE · Father of Indian self-rule · Akkalkot devotee lineage
📖 Holy Text: Historical context linking Sripada Charitamrutam (Book 1) → Akkalkot Swami Samarth → Tilak devotee lineage → Sai Satcharitra era (Book 3)
🔗 How mentioned: Not named in the texts. Tilak's family were devotees of Akkalkot Swami Samarth — the saint prophesied in Book 1. His close associate Dadasaheb Khaparde was a primary Shirdi devotee named in the Sai Satcharitra (Book 3). Tilak's own Gita Rahasya was written in the same 1910–1915 period that Hemadpant was recording Sai Baba's teachings — the Datta tradition and India's independence movement were alive in the same generation.
⏳ Time gap: Sripada prophecy of Shivaji circa 1340 CE → Shivaji 1630 CE → Shivaji Jayanti revived by Tilak 1895 CE — ~555 years: Tilak's revival of Shivaji's legacy completes a cultural arc begun by Sripada's prophecy
Tilak's works: Gita Rahasya (1915), written in Mandalay Prison, is one of the most important modern Gita commentaries — interpreting it as a text of karma yoga and action. Tilak institutionalised the public Ganapati festival (1893) and Shivaji Jayanti, both deeply connected to the Maratha-Datta cultural inheritance.
Dadasaheb Khaparde
1854–1938 CE · Named in Sai Satcharitra · Advocate, Nationalist, Sai devotee
📖 Holy Text: Shri Sai Satcharitra (Book 3) — directly named and his family's miracle recorded
🔗 How mentioned: The Sai Satcharitra records Sai Baba's special affection for Dadasaheb and his wife Lakshmibai. The text narrates the miraculous healing of their young son from plague through Sai Baba's Udi (sacred ash) — one of the most cited healing episodes in the entire Satcharitra. Dadasaheb's extended residence at Shirdi in 1910–1911 and his detailed diary are primary historical sources for the Shirdi period.
⏳ Time gap: Shirdi visits recorded 1910–1911 CE → Sai Satcharitra completed 1929–1930 CE → first English translation 1944 CE — ~19–34 years; Khaparde's presence is living memory within the Satcharitra tradition
His significance: As a member of the Imperial Legislative Council and Privy Council advocate — and Tilak's close associate in the Indian National Congress — Khaparde represents the moment when India's most prominent public figures were also Datta-lineage devotees. The overlap of the freedom movement and the Shirdi tradition in a single person is historically remarkable.
Exploring Parallels Between Spiritual Wisdom and Modern Science
10 Concepts

These sacred texts were composed between the 14th and early 20th centuries. The parallels with modern science noted here are offered as exploratory observations — areas of potential resonance between ancient spiritual wisdom and contemporary scientific inquiry. They are not presented as proof, as scientific conclusions, or as medical advice. The two traditions emerge from very different frameworks and should not be treated as equivalent. Readers are warmly invited to reflect on these resonances with their own discernment.

Where dates of composition are noted alongside modern scientific discoveries, this is historical context only — it is not a claim that these sacred texts predicted, anticipated, or were ahead of modern science. The texts were written for spiritual purposes, in a spiritual framework, by and for people seeking the Divine — not as scientific treatises.

Paramanu — Atomic & Quantum Theory
📖 Sripada Charitamrutam (14th c.) · Exploratory parallel: Quantum Physics
What the text says: Sripada describes reality as composed of Paramanu (पर-अणु, "beyond the smallest") — units of consciousness-infused energy that are neither fully material nor fully immaterial. The paramanu is described as the irreducible basis of creation, vibrating with spiritual potency. Exploratory parallel: Quantum physics established (1920s–present) that subatomic particles exist in superposition — multiple states simultaneously — and "collapse" into one state upon measurement. This offers a thought-provoking philosophical resonance with the Paramanu concept. It is important to note, however, that quantum "observation" refers to physical measurement by instruments, not conscious awareness in the human sense. The parallel is a suggestive analogy, not a scientific equivalence. Time between traditional composition and modern physics: ~600 years.
Time Dilation Across Planes of Existence
📖 Sripada Charitamrutam & Gurucharitra · Exploratory parallel: Relativity
What the text says: Both texts describe multiple planes of existence where time flows at different rates. The Puranic formula embedded in both: "One day of Brahma = 4,320,000,000 human years." The texts describe saints entering states where subjective time moves very slowly relative to external time — tapas lasting "moments" in Brahmaloka but years on earth. Exploratory parallel: Einstein's Special and General Relativity (1905–1916) established that time is not absolute — it passes more slowly under strong gravity or at high velocity. The ancient multi-temporal cosmology and the subjective experience of altered time in deep meditation offer an intriguing resonance with these findings. The two frameworks — traditional cosmological accounts and measured relativistic physics — emerge from very different conceptual traditions and should not be treated as equivalent. Time between traditional composition and modern physics: ~600 years for Charitamrutam; ~500 years for Gurucharitra.
Non-local Consciousness — Bilocation & Entanglement
📖 All Three Texts · Exploratory parallel: Quantum Non-locality
What the texts say: Sripada, Narasimha Saraswati, and Sai Baba all demonstrate bilocation — appearing physically in two locations simultaneously, knowing events happening at a distance without communication, and acting on people miles away. The texts establish consciousness as fundamentally non-local — not confined to the body. These are received within the tradition as accounts of divine grace, not as natural phenomena. Exploratory parallel: Quantum entanglement (Bell's theorem, confirmed 1982 by Aspect) demonstrates that two entangled particles are correlated across any distance. It is important to note that quantum entanglement does not transmit information and does not establish bilocation, distant awareness, or telepathy — which are distinct phenomena. Some consciousness researchers (Penrose-Hameroff, Dean Radin) have explored whether non-local correlations may extend to awareness; this remains an open and contested area of inquiry. The resonance with spiritual accounts is suggestive but not scientifically established. Time between traditional composition and modern physics: 500–600 years.
Vibrational Practice — Mantra and Sound
📖 Gurucharitra & Sripada Charitamrutam · Exploratory parallel: Cymatics & Acoustic Research
What the texts say: The Gurucharitra describes devotional accounts of healing associated with the "Datta mantra." Sripada describes mantra recitation as spiritually active. Within the tradition, these healings are understood as expressions of divine grace, with the mantra as a vehicle of devotion. Exploratory parallel: Cymatics (Hans Jenny, 1960s) demonstrated that sound frequencies can organise matter into geometric patterns. Some research has explored the physiological effects of chanting and meditation, including neurological changes. This research is preliminary and specific studies should be verified individually. Mantra practice as described in these texts should not be understood as a clinical treatment or a replacement for medical care. Devotional healing accounts are presented here within their own spiritual framework. Time between traditional composition and modern research: 500–600 years.
Prana / Bioelectric Field — The Body's Energy System
📖 All Three Texts · Exploratory parallel: Bioelectromagnetics
What the texts say: All three texts describe the body as animated by Prana (प्राण) — a subtle energy force circulating through 72,000 Nadis (channels). Sai Baba's healing touch is described as a "transfer of prana" — His hand placed on a devotee's body transmitting a vitalising force. These accounts are received within the tradition as expressions of divine grace. Exploratory parallel: Modern bioelectromagnetics has confirmed that the body generates measurable electromagnetic fields (the heart's field is measurable by SQUID magnetometers). The HeartMath Institute has researched how this field may affect interpersonal physiology. Fascia research has expanded understanding of the body as an interconnected system. These are distinct frameworks from the subtle-body model of the texts, and the physiological findings do not confirm the Prana or Nadi system as described. The resonance is offered as a point of reflection, not as anatomical equivalence. Time between traditional composition and modern research: 500–600 years.
Pancha Bhuta and modern classifications of matter
📖 Gurucharitra · Exploratory parallel: States of Matter in Physics
What the text says: The Gurucharitra (following Vedic cosmology) describes creation through Pancha Bhuta — five elements: Prithvi (earth / solid), Jala (water / liquid), Agni (fire / energy), Vayu (air / gas), and Akasha (space / ether). These function as cosmological and spiritual categories within the tradition. Exploratory parallel: Modern physics recognises several states of matter: solid, liquid, gas, and plasma. Plasma — often overlooked — is in fact the most common form of visible matter in the universe (stars are plasma), offering an interesting resonance with Agni's significance in the texts. It is important to note that the Pancha Bhuta system and modern states-of-matter classification emerged from entirely different conceptual frameworks and are not structurally identical. Akasha, for example, carries meanings of consciousness and ether that differ substantially from the quantum vacuum or Bose-Einstein condensate, which are precise physical concepts. The parallel is offered as a cultural and philosophical reflection. Time between traditional composition and modern physics: ~500 years (Gurucharitra); the underlying Vedic system is 3,000+ years old.
The Nadi Network — 72,000 Channels and Neural Architecture
📖 Gurucharitra · Exploratory parallel: Neuroscience & Fascia Research
What the text says: The Gurucharitra (following yogic anatomy) describes 72,000 Nadis (subtle channels) carrying prana through the body. The three primary nadis — Ida, Pingala, Sushumna — run along the spine and control physical and mental function. This is a subtle-body map, not a claim of anatomy in the modern scientific sense. Exploratory parallel: The human nervous system contains approximately 86 billion neurons with ~100 trillion synaptic connections. The spinal cord is the primary relay for all nerve signals between brain and body. The sympathetic (activating) and parasympathetic (calming) nervous systems run along the spine. These offer a suggestive structural resonance with Ida, Pingala, and Sushumna — though the yogic and neuroscientific frameworks are distinct and should not be treated as equivalent or precisely corresponding. The number 72,000 Nadis is a traditional figure whose relationship to any anatomical count is not established. Time between traditional composition and modern neuroscience: ~500 years.
Udi — Sacred Ash and Its Significance
📖 Sai Satcharitra (early 20th c.) · Devotional tradition
What the text says: Sai Baba used Udi — ash from the perpetual wood-fire dhuni — as a vehicle of His blessing and grace. He gave it to devotees mixed in water, applied it topically, and sent it by post for distant healing. The Satcharitra records many accounts of recovery from serious illness among those who received it with deep faith. Within the tradition, the Udi's power is understood as the grace of the Sadguru — not as a property of the ash itself. An important distinction: Ordinary wood ash is not equivalent to medical-grade activated charcoal, which is a specifically processed substance used in clinical settings for poisoning and overdose. The two should not be confused. The healing accounts in the Satcharitra are received as expressions of divine grace operating through a sacred object, and are not to be understood as evidence of the ash's chemical properties. Udi is a sacred tradition of the Shirdi Datta Parampara — it is not a medical treatment and should not be used as one.
Faith and the Placebo-Nocebo Effect
📖 All Three Texts · Exploratory parallel: Psychoneuroimmunology
What the texts say: All three texts contain accounts in which the depth of a devotee's faith is associated with the degree of blessing received. The texts present faith as an openness to divine grace — a receptivity rather than a cause. The Sadgurus are presented as giving unconditionally; a devotee's inner state is described as shaping their own capacity to receive. Exploratory parallel: Psychoneuroimmunology (established 1975 by Robert Ader) has demonstrated that belief produces measurable changes in immune function, hormone levels, and neurological pathways. Placebo studies show genuine physiological effects from treatment the patient believes to be real. This is an interesting area of resonance with spiritual teachings on faith and openness. An important distinction: This material should not be read as implying that illness is caused by insufficient faith, or that failure to recover reflects a failure of belief. Such an interpretation would be both theologically incorrect within the Datta tradition — where grace is given freely — and potentially harmful. The Sadgurus' teaching is that grace is ever-present; the spiritual journey is about opening to it, not earning it. Time between traditional composition and modern research: 500–600 years.
Consciousness Beyond the Body — Spiritual Perspectives
📖 All Three Texts · Exploratory parallel: Near-Death Experience Research
What the texts say: All three Sadgurus explicitly teach that consciousness is not produced by the body — it pre-exists, inhabits, and survives it. Sai Baba declared before his mahanirvana: "I shall be active and vigorous from My tomb." Miracles at the Shirdi Samadhi have been reported by devotees from 1918 to the present day. Sripada "disappeared" — his body was never found — consistent with the tradition's teaching that realised consciousness transcends the body entirely. These are received within the Datta tradition as expressions of the living presence of the Sadgurus, not as empirical claims about biology. Exploratory parallel: Near-Death Experience research offers a thought-provoking area of inquiry. Dr. Pim van Lommel's prospective study (Lancet, 2001) and the AWARE study (Dr. Sam Parnia) examined accounts of conscious experience reported by patients during cardiac arrest. An important distinction: cardiac arrest, while life-threatening, is not equivalent to irreversible brain death; resuscitation is possible, and brain activity during such events is not fully understood. These studies are actively debated in the scientific community and do not constitute proof of consciousness surviving death. They are, however, serious scientific investigations that keep the question open — resonant in a general way with the spiritual teachings, though the two speak from very different frameworks. Time between traditional composition and modern research: 500–600 years.
Prophecies and Their Traditional Interpretations — Across All Three Books
12 Prophecies

The sacred texts present these as specific, named, dated, and geographically located statements — recorded before the subjects' births, in some cases by centuries. Within the Datta Parampara tradition, they are received as literal fulfilments rather than allegories. Time-gap figures are based on traditional dating of the texts; where possible, they are cross-referenced with historical records, though some dates remain traditional estimates. Readers are invited to engage with this material in the spirit of the tradition.

Prophecy made: circa 1340 CE · Fulfilled: circa 1378 CE · Gap: ~40 years
🔱 Sripada's Prophecy of Narasimha Saraswati (Book 1 → Book 2)
Source: Sripada Srivallabha Charitamrutam
Sripada explicitly declared: "I am the same as Narasimha Saraswati. I will appear again, born in a Brahmin family in Karanja (Maharashtra). My mother will be Ambabhavani." Narasimha Saraswati was born at Karanja (Lad), Maharashtra, circa 1378 CE — exactly matching the prophecy's location and family description. The birth was accompanied by the same extraordinary signs as Sripada's own birth. Within the tradition, this is presented as the clearest case of a named-location, named-parentage prophecy whose subsequent fulfilment is independently attested.
Prophecy made: circa 1340 CE · Fulfilled: circa 1595 CE · Gap: ~255 years
🛕 Sripada's Prophecy of Raghavendra Swami at Mantralayam (Book 1)
Source: Sripada Srivallabha Charitamrutam, Chs. 20–21
Sripada described a future great Vaishnava saint who would establish his brindavana (living samadhi) on the banks of the Tungabhadra river at a place called Mantralayam. Raghavendra Swami (1595–1671 CE) — philosopher, scholar, and saint — entered his living samadhi at Mantralayam on the Tungabhadra in 1671 CE. Mantralayam remains one of the most visited pilgrimage sites in South India, exactly fulfilling Sripada's geographic specification made 330 years earlier.
Prophecy made: circa 1340 CE · Fulfilled: circa 1700–1878 CE · Gap: ~350–540 years
🔱 Sripada's Prophecy of Swami Samarth at Akkalkot (Book 1)
Source: Sripada Srivallabha Charitamrutam
Sripada named Akkalkot (Solapur district, Maharashtra) as a future seat of the Datta parampara where the third avatar would manifest and bless multitudes. The text uses the name Prajnapura (city of wisdom) for Akkalkot. Swami Samarth appeared at Akkalkot — claiming to have come "from Kardali Vana" (the same mystical forest where Sripada and Narasimha Saraswati departed). He performed miracles there for decades. His mahanirvana in 1878 CE is historically documented.
Prophecy made: circa 1340 CE · Fulfilled: circa 1835–1918 CE · Gap: ~500 years
🕌 Sripada's Prophecy of Sai Baba of Shirdi (Book 1 → Book 3)
Source: Sripada Srivallabha Charitamrutam
Sripada explicitly stated: "In the future, I will take the form of a divine being at a place called Shirdi in Maharashtra. I will be known as Sai Baba. I will serve God in the form of Allah and bless both Hindus and Muslims equally." He also said he was directing the cosmic being Hanuman to incarnate as a devotee at Shirdi to serve this form. Shirdi Sai Baba appeared approximately 500 years later — a Muslim-Hindu saint at Shirdi, Maharashtra. Within the tradition, this is read as matching the prophecy on three key points: location (Shirdi, Maharashtra), religious universality (serving Hindus and Muslims equally), and the name "Sai" (Persian for "saint/master").
Prophecy made: circa 1340 CE · Fulfilled: c.14th–21st century, ongoing
✝️☪️ Sripada's Vision of World Religious Unity (Book 1)
Source: Sripada Srivallabha Charitamrutam
In the Charitamrutam, Shankar Bhatt receives a vision in which Sripada reveals that Jesus Christ and Prophet Muhammad are manifestations of the same divine Dattatreya consciousness in different cultural contexts. The text explicitly names both figures. This text is traditionally attributed to 14th-century composition — centuries before any interfaith movement. The Charitamrutam's statement that "all authentic spiritual masters draw from the same source" was fulfilled historically through Sai Baba (19th–20th c.) who literally read both the Quran and the Ramayan in his mosque-temple, and is now a central principle of modern interfaith dialogue.
Prophecy made: circa 1459 CE · Fulfilled: 1671 CE → ongoing
📖 Narasimha Saraswati's Prophecy — The Gurucharitra as Living Guru (Book 2)
Source: Gurucharitra, final chapters — Phala Shruti (Fruits of Reading)
Narasimha Saraswati declared before his departure: "I will be present wherever the Gurucharitra is read with faith. The book will perform miracles for its readers as I would perform for devotees in my physical presence." He promised specific results for the 7-day parayan reading. Within the tradition, this promise has been held to be continuously operative for 550+ years. The Gurucharitra parayan practice is one of the most widespread spiritual observances in Maharashtra, with millions of reported accounts of healing and answered prayers that remain active to the present day.
Prophecy made: circa 1910 CE · Fulfilled: October 15, 1918
☀️ Sai Baba's Prediction of His Own Mahanirvana (Book 3)
Source: Sai Satcharitra, Chs. XLI–XLIII; Khaparde's Diary; multiple devotee testimonies
In the months before October 1918, Sai Baba gave increasing specific signs of His departure. He distributed His possessions, told Shama "I am going soon," instructed Lakshmibai Shinde on His final rites, and repeatedly indicated Vijayadasami (Dussehra) as the date. He told devotees: "I will not remain in this body much longer." On Vijayadasami, October 15, 1918 — exactly as indicated — He breathed His last. The date, the manner (leaning on Bayaji, dakshina of 9 rupees given to Lakshmibai), and the passing in Dwarkamai all matched His advance descriptions.
Prophecy made: circa 1915 CE · Fulfilled: post-October 1918, ongoing
🏛️ Sai Baba's Prophecy — Booty Wada Becomes His Samadhi (Book 3)
Source: Sai Satcharitra, Chs. XXXIX, XLIII
Sai Baba told G.K. Booty (the merchant building a guesthouse at Shirdi): "I will come and dwell in the Murlidhar temple in your wada." During his entire lifetime, Sai Baba never entered Booty Wada. After His mahanirvana, when a dispute arose over how to inter His body, Sai Baba appeared to devotee Ramachandra Dada Patel in a dream and said: "Place me in Booty's wada." His body was interred in the Murlidhar temple room — the exact location He had named. The Booty Wada became the Samadhi Mandir, now visited by millions annually.
Prophecy made: circa 1918 CE · Fulfilled: 1918–present, over 100 years
⭐ Sai Baba's Eternal Presence Promise (Book 3)
Source: Sai Satcharitra, Chs. XLIV–XLVII; ongoing documentation
Sai Baba explicitly said: "I shall be active and vigorous even from My tomb. My bones shall give hope and confidence to My devotees. Whoever surrenders to Me with whole-hearted devotion, I shall carry the burden of their existence." Within the tradition, this is understood not as metaphor but as a literal operational promise — and it is in this spirit that devotees have lived with it since 1918. Reported accounts of Sai Baba appearing in dreams with specific instructions, intervening in accidents, and healing the sick have continued without interruption for over 100 years. The Shirdi Sai Sansthan (the trust managing the shrine) holds hundreds of thousands of such testimonies.
Prophecy made: circa 1910–1918 CE · Fulfilled: 1941–1969 CE
🌟 Sai Baba's Transmission to Upasani → Meher Baba (Book 3)
Source: Sai Satcharitra, Chs. XXIX–XXX (Upasani Maharaj); supplementary accounts for the Meher Baba lineage
Sai Baba explicitly transmitted his power to Kashinath Govind Upasani Baba, saying: "I have handed the key of my treasury to Upasani — whatever he gives, I am responsible." Upasani Maharaj later transmitted to Meher Baba (Merwan Sheriar Irani). Meher Baba maintained complete silence from 1925 until his death in 1969, saying: "I will break my silence before I die — and when I speak, the world will hear." This transmission chain — Sripada → Narasimha Saraswati → Swami Samarth → Sai Baba → Upasani → Meher Baba — forms the longest historically documented continuous spiritual lineage in the modern era.
Prophecy made: circa 1340 CE · Fulfilled: 1947 CE · Gap: ~607 years
🇮🇳 Sripada's Vision of Bharata Mata's Liberation (Book 1)
Source: Sripada Srivallabha Charitamrutam
The Charitamrutam contains several passages where Sripada describes the future of Bharatavarsha (India) — including a time of "great darkness and foreign domination" followed by a "rising of the light from within the land itself." Devotee-scholars in the independence movement (including Dadasaheb Khaparde, a prominent Congress figure and Sai Baba devotee) read these passages as prophetic of colonial occupation and eventual independence. India's independence in 1947 — 607 years after Sripada's time — is seen within the parampara tradition as the fulfilment of this broad historical vision.
Prophecy made: circa 1878–1918 CE · Ongoing fulfilment
🕊️ Sai Baba's Prophecy of Universal Reach (Book 3)
Source: Sai Satcharitra
Sai Baba told Das Ganu: "My name will spread everywhere. People from all over the world will come to Shirdi." In Sai Baba's lifetime, Shirdi was a remote village accessible only by bullock cart from Kopargaon. The idea that it would become a global pilgrimage site was implausible. Today, the Shirdi Sai Sansthan (the trust) handles 25,000–75,000 daily visitors, over 10 million annually. Sai temples exist in every continent. The Sai Baba movement is one of the fastest-growing devotional traditions in the world — His image is found in homes across India regardless of religion. The prophecy was fulfilled beyond any scale imaginable in His time.
♾️
The Immortal Soul — Revealed by the Omniscient Sadguru
Across all three sacred texts, the Sadguru is described as doing something that shatters ordinary understanding of time: He reveals past births of persons standing before Him and prophesies births yet to come — sometimes centuries ahead. Within the tradition, this is understood not as metaphor but as omniscience made manifest — a Sadguru who stands outside time, seeing the soul's entire journey across incarnations. Every soul journey presented here appears in one of the three holy texts, spanning recorded intervals of up to 600 years between prophecy and apparent fulfilment.
🔭
PAST BIRTHS REVEALED
The Sadguru tells a devotee about their previous incarnations — sometimes many lives back — to explain the karmic context of their present situation.
🔮
FUTURE BIRTHS FORETOLD
The Sadguru prophesies where a soul will be born in its next incarnation — sometimes giving the name, place, century, and life's mission — centuries before it happens.
🧵
SOUL GROUPS TRAVEL TOGETHER
Souls that were companions in one era are prophesied to be reborn together — as warriors, saints, and spiritual masters — fulfilling a collective divine plan across centuries.
🌟 THE AVATAR'S OWN SOUL ARC — Across All Three Holy Texts
📖 All Three Books
Dattatreya's Own Incarnation Chain — The Spine of the Entire Parampara
Divine Source
Lord Dattatreya
Eternal
→
1st Incarnation
Sripada Srivallabha
1320–1350 CE · Pithapuram
→
2nd Incarnation
Narasimha Saraswati
1378–1459 CE · Karanjapuram
→
3rd Incarnation
Akkalkot Swami Samarth
~1778–1878 CE · Akkalkot
📖 Declared In: Sripada Srivallabha Charitamrutam (Book 1) — Sripada's own direct proclamation
🔗 Self-Prophecy: "After retracing My incarnation as Sripada Srivallabha, I will be born as Sri Narasimha Saraswati… He proclaimed himself that He would be reborn two more times, one time as Narasimha Saraswati and another time as Swami Samarth."
⏳ Arc Span: ~1350 CE (Sripada departs) → ~1878 CE (Swami Samarth departs) = 528 years of continuous divine presence
Metaphysical Significance (as understood within the tradition): A living Sadguru publicly announcing His next two incarnations with names, locations, and time-periods — this is among the most striking declarations recorded in Datta Parampara literature. Book 1 (Charitamrutam) records the announcement; Book 2 (Gurucharitra) documents the advent that tradition receives as its fulfilment. The same soul, the same grace, the same omniscience — across three bodies, three centuries, one unbroken stream of Divine Consciousness.
⚔️ THE VATSAVAYI SOUL GROUP — One Circle, Four Centuries, Three Destinies
📖 Book 1 — Charitamrutam
Vatsavayi Narasimha Varma & Brothers → Shivaji · Ramdas · Sridhar
This Birth (circa 1320 CE)
Vatsavayi Narasimha Varma
Kshatriya nobleman, Pithapuram
+ his brothers/associates
→→→
Future Births
Shivaji Maharaj (1630–1680)
+ Samarth Ramdas (1608–1681)
+ Sridhar Swami (1908–1973)
📖 Holy Text: Sripada Srivallabha Charitamrutam (Book 1) — Prophecy of Shivaji by name
🔗 Prophecy: Sripada prophesied that Vatsavayi Narasimha Varma's soul would be reborn as Chhatrapati Shivaji Maharaj — a great Kshatriya king who would establish a Hindu kingdom. His companions would be reborn as Samarth Ramdas and Sridhar Swami — the same soul-group, reunited across 290–568 years to continue their dharmic mission.
⏳ Time Gaps: Narasimha Varma → Shivaji: ~290–310 years · Narasimha Varma → Ramdas: ~290 yrs · → Sridhar Swami: ~588 years
Metaphysical Significance (as received in the tradition): Within the tradition, a Kshatriya nobleman at Pithapuram in 1320 CE is prophesied to return as the great Shivaji Maharaj who founded the Maratha Empire — presented as among the most striking personal prophecies in the Charitamrutam. His soul companions are prophesied to return simultaneously as Ramdas (Shivaji's own Guru) and Sridhar Swami (who would install Sripada's padukas at Pithapuram in the 20th century). The soul-group, in this reading, incarnates together, plays complementary roles, and advances a collective dharmic mission across six centuries.
⚠️ Note: The specific Charitamrutam chapter documenting the brothers' connection to Ramdas and Sridhar requires verification from the printed Telugu/Marathi text. The Shivaji prophecy is widely cited; the Ramdas/Sridhar connection is referenced by devotees and scholars of the tradition but the exact chapter numbers are yet to be confirmed.
📖 Book 1 — Charitamrutam
Samarth Ramdas → Gajanan Maharaj of Shegaon
Birth 1
Samarth Ramdas Swami
1608–1681 CE · Janwad, Maharashtra
→
Rebirth (~197 years later)
Gajanan Maharaj
circa 1878–1910 CE · Shegaon, Maharashtra
📖 Holy Text: Sripada Srivallabha Charitamrutam (Book 1)
🔗 Exact Prophecy: "Samardha Ramdas himself will become famous as Gajanan Maharaj in 'Shiv Gaon' and become an avadhoota."
⏳ Time Gap: Prophecy made circa 1320–1350 CE → Ramdas born 1608 → Gajanan Maharaj appears 1878. From prophecy to final fulfilment: ~528–558 years. From Ramdas's death to Gajanan Maharaj's appearance: ~197 years.
Metaphysical Significance (as received in the tradition): Within the tradition, Sripada prophesied in the 14th century that Samarth Ramdas — who would himself only be born in 1608 — would then be reborn as Gajanan Maharaj at Shegaon. This is understood as prophecy stacked inside prophecy: first Ramdas must come into existence, fulfil his mission as Shivaji's guru, then in his next birth appear as an Avadhuta saint at Shegaon. The place name "Shiv Gaon" is received by tradition as corresponding to present-day Shegaon, Buldhana district.
⚠️ Note: The identification of "Shiv Gaon" with present-day Shegaon in Buldhana district is accepted by the Gajanan Maharaj Sansthan tradition and devotee scholars. The exact Charitamrutam chapter number for this prophecy is pending verification from the printed text.
📜 PREDESTINED SOULS — Their Very Encounter with the Sadguru Was Foretold
📖 Book 1 — Charitamrutam
Shankar Bhatt — The Appointed Biographer
Previous Birth
Born in Mogalicharla
Near Kandukuru, Andhra Pradesh
→
This Birth (circa 1320 CE)
Shankar Bhatt
Brahmin from Karnataka
Author of the Charitamrutam
📖 Holy Text: Sripada Srivallabha Charitamrutam (Book 1) — Chapter 1 and throughout
🔗 How Mentioned: Sripada knew Shankar Bhatt would come before he arrived. The divine padukas were arranged to be given to "persons named Shankar Bhatt and Dharma Gupta." His entire epic journey of thousands of kilometres — not knowing Sripada, then finding Him — was part of a pre-arranged plan. Sripada revealed to Shankar Bhatt that his devotional merit in a previous birth (in Mogalicharla village near Kandukuru) had earned him this unique privilege of recording the divine biography.
Metaphysical Point: Even the author of the sacred text was predestined. The book itself is a karmic fruit — Shankar Bhatt's previous life of devotion earned him the right to write it. The act of writing the Charitamrutam was itself a soul's fulfilment across two lifetimes.
📖 Book 1 — Charitamrutam
The Washerman of Kuruvapuram — Desire Becomes Destiny
This Birth (circa 1320 CE)
An unnamed washerman
At the ghats of Kuruvapuram
Envied a Muslim king's wealth
→
Next Birth (fulfils desire)
Born as a Prince
Royal household at Bidar
(Vaidurya nagar, Karnataka)
📖 Holy Text: Sripada Srivallabha Charitamrutam (Book 1)
🔗 Exact Prophecy: "In your next life you would take birth in the house of a Muslim emperor at Vaidurya nagar (Bidar, Karnataka). There you would enjoy all sorts of royal pleasures. In that birth too you will get my divine blessings."
Metaphysical Significance: Even an ungodly emotion — envy — witnessed in the presence of the Sadguru becomes a karmic blueprint. The washerman's desire was not sinful enough to cause a fall, yet strong enough to create a future birth. The remarkable addendum: "In that birth too you will get my divine blessings" — Sripada promises to continue guiding this soul even in a future incarnation into a Muslim royal dynasty. The Sadguru's grace transcends not only time but all boundaries of religion and caste.
📖 Book 1 — Charitamrutam
Vyaghreshwar — The Tiger Soul Liberated by Contact with the Avatar
Previous Birth
Vyaghreshwar
Wrestler who tortured tigers
for public entertainment
→
This Birth (karmic consequence)
Born as a Tiger
Near Krishna River banks
→
Liberation (this same birth)
Radiant soul emerged
At the moment of death
carrying Sripada across the river
📖 Holy Text: Sripada Srivallabha Charitamrutam — the river-crossing episode
🔗 Text Witness: "All the atoms in the form of the tiger disintegrated and a man with a lustrous celestial body manifested there" — the soul was liberated at the moment it served as the Avatar's vehicle across the river.
Metaphysical Point: Even an animal born in consequences of past karma can achieve liberation through a single act of selfless service to the Avatar. The tiger served as a vehicle for Sripada — one act — and that was enough to end the cycle. This illustrates the Datta teaching that the Avatar's touch is not bound by species, caste, or the apparent weight of karma.
🔗 THE GREAT CROSS-BOOK KARMIC FULFILLMENT — Book 1 Promise → Book 2 Reality
📖 Book 1 → Book 2 · Cross-Text Arc
Amba Bhavani — The Mother Who Was Promised Her Son by the Previous Avatar
Previous Life
Amba Bhavani
Devoted servant of Sripada
(in Book 1 era, circa 1320–1350)
→
Next Life (Book 2 Era)
Amba Bhavani, Mother
Karanjapuram (Karanja, Maharashtra)
circa 1378 CE
→
The Promise Fulfilled
Narasimha Saraswati
Born as her son, 1378 CE
Sripada's reincarnation
📖 Cross-Book Source: Promise in Book 1 (Charitamrutam era) → Fulfilled in Book 2 (Gurucharitra)
🔗 Wikipedia Confirmation: "In her previous birth, Sripada Srivallabha had blessed her & he advised her to perform Shiva Pooja. Later he also told that he would be born to her in her next life as Narasimha Saraswathi to uphold the Sanatha Dharma in Kali Yuga."
⏳ Time Gap: The blessing in Sripada's era (circa 1320–1350) → Narasimha Saraswati born 1378 CE = approximately 28–58 years from promise to fulfilment (a relatively short cross-life span, suggesting Amba Bhavani's devotion was so intense it accelerated her rebirth).
Metaphysical Significance — The Deepest Link Between Books 1 and 2: This is the master karmic thread that weaves the entire Datta sacred library into one tapestry. Book 2 (Gurucharitra) is not just a sequel — it is the living fulfilment of a soul-promise made in Book 1. Amba Bhavani's devotion in her previous birth earned her the supreme boon: the Avatar himself was born as her son. Every miracle, every chapter of Gurucharitra — the entire life of Narasimha Saraswati — is Sripada honouring a promise to a devoted soul. The two books are one unbroken act of divine love spanning two lifetimes.
🐘 TIRUMALA DAS OF KANIPAKAM — The Karmic Gatekeeper (Book 1, Chapter 5)
📖 Book 1 — Charitamrutam · Chapter 5
Previous Birth
Miserly Vedic Scholar
Learned in scriptures
but hard-hearted at death
→
Present Birth
Tirumala Das
Washerman-devotee at Kanipakam
circa 1320s CE (age ~70 when met)
→
The Transformation
Karmic Gatekeeper
Directs Shankar Bhatt
to begin the Charitamrutam
📍 Location: Sri Varasiddhi Vinayaka Temple, Kanipakam, Chittoor district, Andhra Pradesh
🔗 His past life: Tirumala Das reveals to Shankar Bhatt that in his previous birth he was a Vedic scholar — learned in the scriptures, but miserly and hard-hearted. At the moment of death, his mind clung to this miserliness rather than to the divine. As a consequence of that final consciousness, he was reborn as a washerman — a caste of humble physical labour. Yet the Datta Parampara's grace transformed even this: in his washerman birth, Tirumala Das became a devoted servant of Dattatreya, receiving temple prasadam through trained dogs, and was chosen to be the one who set Shankar Bhatt on his mission.
🔗 His role in the Charitamrutam: He is the person who (1) commands Shankar Bhatt to begin writing the biography of Sripada; (2) directs him to seek Vatsavayi Narasimha Varma in Pithapuram — without which the great soul-prophecies in the text would never have been narrated; (3) tells Shankar Bhatt Sripada's childhood stories for the first time; (4) recounts the svayambhu origin of the Kanipakam Vinayaka idol.
Metaphysical Significance — The Karmic Gatekeeper: The Datta Parampara here reveals a profound teaching embedded in narrative structure: the very person who was reborn for his miserliness is the one through whose hands the entire spiritual biography must pass. A washerman — the most unexpected of guides — holds the keys to the most extraordinary sacred text in the Telugu tradition. Sripada's omniscience arranged this: Tirumala Das's very birth as a washerman, which looked like karmic punishment, was in truth his appointment — the Sadguru working through the wheel of karma to place the right soul in the right place at the right moment to launch the writing of His own biography. This teaches that the Sadguru's hand is never absent from the apparent humiliations of karma.

Furthermore, Tirumala Das's soul journey — from learned scholar to devoted washerman — mirrors the Charitamrutam's central teaching: that knowledge without devotion leads to rebirth; devotion without ego, however humble the caste, leads to liberation.
🕊️ SOUL DIMENSIONS IN SAI SATCHARITRA — Book 3
📖 Book 3 — Sai Satcharitra
Hemadpant — Karmic Mission Declared by Sai
Annasaheb Dabholkar (Hemadpant) went to Shirdi to test Sai Baba. Before he could even speak, Sai said: "You will write my story." Hemadpant was shocked — he considered himself unworthy. Yet this became the Sai Satcharitra, one of the most-read books in India. The Sadguru knew the soul's mission before the soul itself did. Hemadpant's entire literary life was a karmic preparation for this one divinely ordained task.
📖 Book 3 — Sai Satcharitra
Mhalsapati — The Soul That Recognised the Avatar
When a young unknown wanderer first entered the Khandoba temple at Shirdi, the priest Mhalsapati spontaneously called out: "Aao Sai!" — "Welcome, Sai!" No one had used that name before. Mhalsapati's soul recognised the divine before the mind could process it. This first utterance of "Sai" gave the Avatar His name. Sai Baba later said Mhalsapati was His "first devotee" in Shirdi — a karmic bond that pre-dated the meeting.
📖 Book 3 — Sai Satcharitra
Nana Saheb Chandorkar — Transformation Across Chapters
Deputy Collector Chandorkar appears repeatedly throughout the Satcharitra — a soul being deliberately shaped and tested by Sai across multiple encounters. His arc from an educated sceptic to a fully surrendered devotee is documented in multiple chapters. Sai Baba told him things about his private life and inner thoughts that only a Sadguru outside time could know — repeatedly demonstrating that He had been watching Chandorkar's soul long before they met.
🌸 SRIPADA'S SACRED FAMILY — Souls Chosen Across Lives to Birth the Avatar (Book 1)
📖 Book 1 — Charitamrutam
Bapannavadhani — Maternal Grandfather of the Avatar
Past Soul
Nanda Maharaja
Krishna's foster father
→
This Birth
Malladi Bapannavadhani
Scholar, Pithapuram
He who had the supreme fortune of being foster-father to Lord Krishna was reborn as the maternal grandfather of the next Dattatreya Avatar. The Charitamrutam declares that the book would reach the world in the 33rd generation of Bapannavadhani's lineage — his soul's service to the divine was not finished at one incarnation but encoded into the DNA of an entire family lineage across six centuries.
📖 Book 1 — Charitamrutam
Appalaraja Sarma — The Father Who Offered Himself Entirely
Past Soul
Vishnu Datta
Pure Datta devotee
→
This Birth
Appalaraja Sarma
Brahmin priest, Pithapuram
Reborn as a humble, wandering Brahmin priest who went house-to-house performing rituals — a life of complete simplicity and surrender. The Charitamrutam records: "He was a great devotee of Datta and offered himself to the divine completely." Sripada's father was not chosen for worldly wealth or power, but for the purity of a soul that had already spent a previous lifetime in total devotional offering. Yogic lights emerged from the eyes of both Appalaraja Sarma and Sumati during deep meditation, combined, and lodged in Sumati's womb — the Avatar was formed from two surrendered souls.
📖 Book 1 — Charitamrutam
Sumati Maharani — The Mother Whose Womb Sheltered the Avatar
Past Soul
Somidevamma
Devoted woman of faith
→
This Birth
Sumati Maharani
Mother of Sripada, Pithapuram
Sumati came from a noble scholarly family, yet she "offered herself to the husband who was very humble — his life, his house," seeing divine righteousness in his simplicity. Her past-life devotion as Somidevamma earned her the greatest boon any soul can receive: to carry the Avatar in her womb. The Charitamrutam presents her not as a passive biographical detail but as a spiritually prepared vessel — a soul who, through accumulated devotion over lifetimes, had earned the right to be the divine mother.
⚠️ Scholarly Note: The specific Charitamrutam chapters for the family past-life connections (Nanda Maharaja, Vishnu Datta, Somidevamma) are cited widely in devotional literature and in-tradition commentary. The exact chapter and verse numbers from the printed Telugu edition are pending direct verification.
🐐 PAST-LIFE KARMA MADE VISIBLE — Sai Baba's Animal Revelations (Book 3, Chs. 46–47)
📖 Book 3 — Sai Satcharitra · Chapter 46
The Two Goats — Brothers Reborn in Enmity
Past Life
Two Uterine Brothers
Companions of Sai in a previous era
→
The Karma (cause)
Mutual Enmity & Violence
Elder tried to kill younger; both died together
→
Present Life
Reborn as Two Goats
In a passing flock near Shirdi
📖 Sai's Own Words (first person): "In their former birth they were human beings and had the good fortune to be My companions and sit by My side. They were uterine brothers, loving each other at first, but later on, they became enemies. The elder brother was an idle fellow while the younger one was an active chap and earned a lot of money. The elder became greedy and jealous and wanted to kill his brother and take away his money. They forgot their fraternal relations and began to quarrel with each other... at last both of them died at the same place and were reborn as these goats."
🔗 Sai's Action: Sai spent Rs. 32 on these two goats from a passing herd-owner — their actual market value was Rs. 7–8. The devotees thought Sai was deceived. Sai revealed the truth: He recognized them, spent lavishly to buy them, and fed them with care — giving them peace in their animal births and the touch of His grace once again, to accelerate their liberation.
Metaphysical Significance: "Mutual enmity causes one to fall from a higher state of life to a lower. Natural evolution of life is destroyed by debt, animosity and murder." (Satcharitra, Chapter 47 commentary). Two souls who had the rarest privilege of being Sai's companions in a previous life squandered it through greed and hatred — and fell from human to animal birth. Yet even as goats, the Sadguru did not abandon them. He recognised them in an ordinary passing flock, paid an enormous price to bring them under His care, and fed them with love. The Sadguru's grace, once given, tracks the soul across species and births — it does not expire.
📖 Book 3 — Sai Satcharitra · Chapter 47
Veerbhadrappa & Chenbassappa — The Snake and the Frog
Past Life — Veerbhadrappa
Corrupt Temple Treasurer
Embezzled temple repair donations
including money of a poor woman
→
Reborn as
A Snake
Still harbouring enmity
Past Life — Dubaki / Chenbassappa
Poor Woman Defrauded
Cheated of her donation by Veerbhadrappa
Sought Sai's protection and refuge
→
Reborn as
A Frog
Still being hunted by old enemy
📖 Sai Narrates in First Person (Chapter 47): "One morning, after taking My breakfast, I strolled along till I came to a small river bank. As I was tired, I rested there. I saw a frog caught by a big snake and was crying. I told [the wayfarer] that both were very wicked in their past life and were now reaping the fruit of their actions in these bodies." Sai called out to the snake by its past-life name — "Veerbhadrappa!" — and asked it to release the frog, reminding it of its past karma. At His words, the snake immediately released the frog, dived into the river, and disappeared. The frog jumped free into the bushes. Sai explained: "Hearing the croaking of Chenbassappa and remembering My pledge [to protect him], I came here, saved him and kept My word."
🔗 Key Detail: Veerbhadrappa had in his human birth been a devotee of Sai too — Sai had even arranged his marriage with Gouri. His betrayal of that divine trust through corruption and cruelty led to his snake birth. Yet even as a snake, Sai's one word — his past-life name — was enough to dissolve the predatory impulse. The debt between the two souls could only be settled in Sai's presence.
Metaphysical Significance: This story, narrated entirely by Sai Baba in the first person, is one of the most intimate revelations in the Satcharitra — the Sadguru acting as cosmic accountant and liberator simultaneously. Greed, corruption with sacred donations, and betrayal of one who trusted you — these are the specific karmas that created animal births. The Sadguru tracked both souls across their deaths and rebirths, honoured His ancient promise to the wronged soul, and with one word dissolved centuries of karmic enmity. The lesson: no debt — of grace or of sin — goes unresolved in the presence of the Sadguru.
📖 Book 3 — Sai Satcharitra · Chapter 7
Bhagoji Shinde — The Leper Chosen to Serve the Avatar's Hand
Past Life
A Sinner
Also accumulated merits of service
→
Karmic Fruit
Born with Leprosy
Visible suffering of past karma
→
The Grace (past merits fruit)
Chosen to tend Sai's hand
The only person Sai permitted
to daily bind His wound
📖 Text Source: Sai Satcharitra, Chapter 7 — On Sai's festering hand wound: Sai had full and exclusive control over who could touch and bandage His hand. Of all the devotees present, He chose Bhagoji Shinde — a man visibly afflicted with leprosy — for this daily intimate service. The Satcharitra reveals the karmic reason: Bhagoji was a sinner in his past birth but had also accumulated sufficient merits. His leprosy was the punishment for the sins; his appointment as the Avatar's personal hand-servant was the fruit of those merits — both played out simultaneously in a single lifetime.
Metaphysical Significance: This story embodies the most subtle teaching in all the Satcharitra: that karma is not simply punishment or reward — it is precision. Bhagoji carried both sin and merit from his past life into this one birth, and both were redeemed simultaneously. His leprosy — visible, humbling, socially isolating — burned off the karma of past sins. His service to Sai's physical hand — intimate, daily, trusted — was the flowering of past merits. The Avatar chose the most socially marginal person present to perform the most intimate act of service. The Sadguru reversed the social order entirely: the leper was more spiritually prepared than any healthy, educated devotee standing nearby.
🙏
The Master Teaching: What These Soul Journeys Reveal
Taken together, these documented soul journeys across the three texts constitute a systematic metaphysical revelation: the soul is immortal; death is a doorway, not an ending; desires, devotion, and service create karmic blueprints that play out across centuries; and the Sadguru — standing outside of time — sees the entire arc of every soul's journey simultaneously.

The fact that prophecies made in 1320 CE were fulfilled with named persons, places, and life-missions 200–600 years later is not mythology — it is documented history that invites us to ask: what is the nature of the consciousness that can perceive across six centuries? That question is the beginning of real spiritual inquiry. These texts do not ask us to believe. They ask us to witness the evidence and draw our own conclusions.
ॐ श्री दत्ताय नमः · श्रीपाद राजम् शरणम् प्रपद्ये
About Shri Swami Samartha Akkalkot

🔱 The Divine Subject

Shri Swami Samartha of Akkalkot is the third great Dattatreya Avatar of Kali Yuga — directly prophesied in the Shri Gurucharitra by Narasimha Saraswati Himself. Known by many names — Akkalkot Niwasi, Swami Maharaj, Swami Samartha — He appeared at the city gate of Akkalkot (ancient name: Prajnapura) in Sholapur district, Maharashtra, in Shake 1779 (1856 CE), on the auspicious day of Ashwin Shuddha Panchami. His identity as a Dattatreya incarnation was confirmed by His own statement: "I am Narasimha Saraswati" — the unbroken continuity of the parampara made explicit.

📖 The Sacred Biography

The biography — Shri Swami Samartha Akkalkot — was compiled from the oral testimonies, written accounts, and direct experiences of His close disciples. It documents His arrival at Akkalkot, His 21 miraculous years there, and His Mahasamadhi. The text is structured thematically: each section focuses on a category of leela — healing, liberation, testing devotees, humbling the proud, blessing the humble — building a comprehensive portrait of a living Dattatreya Avatar operating in 19th-century India.

🕉️ The Divine Lineage

Nrusimhabhan — a Yajurvedi Brahmin of Kashyap Gotra — first recognised the arriving saint and gave Him shelter. Swami Samartha came from the dense Kadali Van (plantain forest) region, the same sacred forest into which Narasimha Saraswati had disappeared in 1459 CE. The biographical tradition holds that Swami Samartha is Narasimha Saraswati Himself — returning after four centuries of invisible presence, now manifesting again in human form at the precise location prophesied: Prajnapura (Akkalkot).

🌺 The Akkalkot Years (21 Years)

Swami Samartha resided at Akkalkot for approximately 21 years — from Shake 1779 (1856 CE) to His Mahasamadhi on Chaitra Shuddha Trayodashi, Shake 1800 (1878 CE). During these years He performed hundreds of documented miracles: healing blindness, deafness, paralysis and leprosy; reviving the dead; blessing the childless with sons; protecting devotees in legal, financial and physical danger; and liberating souls regardless of caste, religion or social status. His presence at Akkalkot transformed it into one of the great pilgrimage centres of Maharashtra.

⭐ Central Teachings

Unlike the textual Gurus of earlier centuries, Swami Samartha rarely delivered formal discourses. His teaching was entirely through action, presence, and direct intervention in His devotees' lives. His behaviour was often unpredictable — now gentle and parental, now fierce and testing. The consistent thread: "I belong to My devotee." He demonstrated by example that caste, religion, and social status are irrelevant to divine grace. A Muslim businessman, a Hindu revolutionary, a Christian devotee — all received His equal attention. His final message was the Swaroop Sampradaya: the Guru's presence continues through His disciples after Mahasamadhi.

🛕 Mahasamadhi

Swami Samartha took Mahasamadhi on Chaitra Shuddha Trayodashi, Shake 1800 (1878 CE). Before departing, He gave various indications to His close disciples — an empty palanquin, cryptic statements, and blessings to those who would not see Him again in that body. His principal disciple Cholappa was present. The samadhi mandir at Akkalkot became, and remains, one of the most visited pilgrimage sites in Maharashtra. The Swami's promise, repeated often during His lifetime: "I will never abandon My devotees. Even after this body, I am present wherever My name is called."
