Chapter Summaries — Shri Swami Samartha Akkalkot
24 Sections
Section 1 ✦
Manifestation & Early Life
The divine appearance of Swami Samartha at the gates of Akkalkot city on Ashwin Shuddha Panchami, Shake 1779 (1856 CE). Nrusimhabhan, a devout Yajurvedi Brahmin, first encounters the wandering saint and provides shelter. The early days at Akkalkot — devotees drawn instinctively to His presence, the first recognitions of His divine nature, and the confirmation of His identity as a Dattatreya incarnation. His coming from the Kadali Van fulfils Narasimha Saraswati's ancient prophecy in the Gurucharitra. The gathering of His first circle of devotees and the beginning of the miraculous leelas that would continue for 21 years.
Section 2 ✦
Journey from Kadali Van
The sacred tradition that Swami Samartha emerged from the Kadali Van — the same plantain forest near Srisailam where Narasimha Saraswati had disappeared from public view in 1459 CE. The biographical account describes His journey northward through the Deccan plateau. Where He stayed, what signs appeared along the route, and the testimony of those who encountered Him before Akkalkot. The seamless continuity of the Datta Avatar tradition: the forest swallowed one form in 1459 CE, and four centuries later released another form at Akkalkot — the parampara flowing beneath the visible surface of history like an underground river.
Section 3 ✦
Early Akkalkot Miracles
The initial cluster of leelas that established Swami Samartha's divinity in the minds of Akkalkot's residents. A Chinese couple seeking business success receive His blessing and return prosperous. A deer fleeing hunters finds shelter at His feet — Swami Samartha grants it protection, and the hunters are unable to approach. Tigers in the region bow to Him during a forest walk. These early miracles — across species, across nationalities, across the usual boundaries of "who gets divine grace" — immediately signal that this is not an ordinary saint but a universal presence beyond all human categories.
Section 4 ✦
Healing the Crippled
Multiple accounts of Swami Samartha healing paralysis and physical disability — in forms ranging from complete limb restoration to gradual recovery through His instruction. The cripple from Hanumandhari village crawls to Swami Samartha on hands and knees; with a single touch or glance, he stands and walks. The cases of two Brahmin lepers whose affliction was not merely physical but karmic — Swami Samartha addressed both the karma and the body simultaneously. The biographical record emphasises that His healings were not random acts of power but precise interventions at the intersection of karmic readiness and divine grace.
Section 5 ✦
Healing the Dumb
The restoration of speech to those born dumb or rendered mute by illness or shock. The case of a dumb Brahmin who had never spoken a word in his life — Swami Samartha places His hand on the man's throat and commands speech. The Brahmin immediately begins chanting Sanskrit shlokas. Manyaba, a devoted woman who had been mute for years, receives speech after Swami Samartha places vibhuti (sacred ash) on her tongue. Each case in this section illuminates the connection between speech (Vak Shakti) and the Guru's power — the same Guru who is identified with the Logos, the creative sound that underlies all manifestation.
Section 6 ✦
Restoring Eyesight
The remarkable cluster of sight-restoration miracles: Chimatai Raste, the wife of a prominent Pune family, is instructed by Swami Samartha to apply cat's milk to her eyes — a seemingly absurd remedy that instantly cures her blindness. Nagoo Anna, a prominent lawyer, travels to Akkalkot in desperation after losing his sight; Swami Samartha restores it. A blind Sanskrit Pandit receives sight when Swami Samartha places a garland of flowers on his head. The young woman Janaki whose congenital blindness is cured. Each case documents not just the miracle but the specific instruction or gesture through which the healing occurred — the biography's precision is striking.
Section 7 ✦
Liberation From Calamities
Divine intervention in legal and financial disasters. Nathoba, a government treasurer, is falsely accused of embezzlement and faces ruin. He prays to Swami Samartha — that night, Swami Samartha appears in a dream to the judge, who dismisses the case the next morning. Rain falls at the Jehur temple during a severe drought — after the local villagers pray through Swami Samartha's name. Entire Vaishnava villages threatened with collective punishment are released from their charge through Swami Samartha's intervention. Pandu Goldsmith, Chintopant Tol, Bhausaheb Jahagirdar — each faces a specific calamity; each is liberated precisely and without waste.
Section 8 ✦
Earnest Desire for Offspring
The great theme of childlessness — one of the most common reasons devotees sought Swami Samartha's grace. Bhausaheb Jahagirdar, a prominent landowner, despairs of an heir after years of childless marriage; Swami Samartha blesses him and a son is born. A childless couple asks for a miracle — Swami Samartha places His hand on a dry tree and two eggs appear from the dried dates; the couple are told they will have two children. A woman from the Mahar community (considered untouchable in 19th-century Maharashtra) approaches Swami Samartha in the same line as Brahmin nobles — and receives the same grace, a son. King Maloji Raje of Akkalkot receives the blessing of an heir through Swami Samartha's direct intervention.
Section 9 ✦
Protected By God
Accounts of divine protection from physical death and mortal danger. A Brahmin's son, bitten by a venomous serpent, is brought to Swami Samartha — He speaks to the boy and life returns. Ravanna Vani, a merchant, is bitten by a cobra while traveling; at the moment of the bite, Swami Samartha (hundreds of miles away) is seen to wince — and the merchant recovers without treatment. Bahireshastri's young son falls into a well; he is recovered alive and uninjured. Hanumant, suffering from a severe fever that no physician could treat, is healed after Swami Samartha instructs him to use neem leaves. Even a dead mouse is brought back to life before the eyes of witnesses — the biography records this to establish: no form of death is outside His reach.
Section 10 ✦
Take Off the Veil of Illusion
Episodes in which Swami Samartha directly dismantles a devotee's false self-image or spiritual pride. The most striking: Mate Buwa, a respected local religious leader, arrives at Akkalkot convinced of his own holiness. Swami Samartha ignores him completely, lavishes attention on a street sweeper who has followed Mate Buwa there, and finally says to Mate Buwa — in front of everyone — "Your robes are very clean but what do they cover?" Mate Buwa collapses in tears; this was the moment of his genuine surrender. The section documents multiple such encounters where Swami Samartha's apparent rudeness was actually surgical precision — removing the specific obstruction that stood between a soul and genuine liberation.
Section 11 ✦
Improper Behaviour
Accounts of Swami Samartha's deliberate unpredictability — the avadhuta's freedom from social convention used as a teaching instrument. He might throw His plate of food at a wall, speak apparent obscenities, demand behavior from devotees that made no conventional sense, or appear in dreams in alarming forms. The biography carefully contextualises each episode: His "improper" behavior consistently targeted a specific rigidity or attachment in the devotee or observer. A hypocritical alchemist who arrives to demonstrate his powers is simply asked to leave — by being ignored. A Shankaracharya (head of a major monastery) who arrives expecting deference is made to wait for hours in the sun before Swami Samartha finally acknowledges him — with a hug and a smile, as if no time had passed.
Section 12 ✦
Outward Show
The testing of those who come for display rather than devotion. Three ascetics — each with impressive external signs of renunciation: matted hair, ash-covered bodies, elaborate ritual objects — arrive at Akkalkot seeking recognition. Swami Samartha speaks to their inner state, not their outer appearance, and each is exposed: one has enormous attachment to his reputation, another harbors deep resentment, the third has never sincerely practiced what he preaches. A group of Vaishnava Brahmins arrive with ceremonial grandeur — Swami Samartha tests each one and finds the simplest among them, an illiterate cook, most worthy of blessing. The section establishes His central teaching: the Divine sees the interior, never the performance.
Section 13 ✦
Mischievous People
Episodes involving those who come to mock, challenge, or expose Swami Samartha as a fraud. Each encounter ends with the sceptic's conversion or public humiliation — not through argument but through direct demonstration. The Shankaracharya episode in its full form: the learned head of a monastic institution arrives determined to intellectually expose a village saint. He leaves a devoted disciple. The episode at Kotikoop near Rameshwar: a local priest denies Swami Samartha access to the temple, citing purity rules; Swami Samartha circumambulates the temple wall, and the locked doors open by themselves. The story of Ganesh's eyesight — a local skeptic loses his sight and regains it only after his sincere apology.
Section 14 ✦
Miracles
A dedicated section of pure miraculous accounts without specific thematic categorisation. Pollution appearing in a government officer's (Sohoni's) house disappears after Swami Samartha's visit. A she-monkey named Sundari — wild and feared in the area — is tamed by a single gesture from Swami Samartha and becomes gentle for the rest of her life. A startled elephant about to cause a stampede in Akkalkot's bazaar is calmed by Swami Samartha walking toward it calmly. A spice-box lost in a well reappears on the kitchen shelf. Footprints appear permanently on a wooden seat. At the Jangam Muth, 4,000 pilgrims are fed from provisions prepared for 40 — the food multiplies visibly. Vamanbua is saved from drowning by an invisible hand.
Section 15 ✦
I Belong To the Devotee
The central theological teaching of the entire biography expressed through direct leelas: Swami Samartha's total belonging to the sincere devotee, without hierarchy, without reservation. Vishnupant, a minor government official, is about to lose his position through false accusation — Swami Samartha intervenes at the exact moment of the judge's verdict. Vithabai, a simple household cook, feeds 4,000 pilgrims from her kitchen — the food multiplying because Swami Samartha Himself sat in her kitchen that day. Moharram festival rings lost in a river are retrieved. Kanphata Eknath, a wandering devotee of another tradition, is saved from certain death. The pattern across all these leelas: Swami Samartha's grace flows most freely where the need is most urgent and the surrender most complete.
Section 16 ✦
Tutelary Deity
The philosophical and practical question of Ishta Devata (chosen or tutelary deity) addressed through Swami Samartha's encounters with devotees from different traditions. A Vaishnava devotee worried that honoring a Shaiva saint violates his tradition — Swami Samartha's response: "The Deity you worship and the Guru before you are the same consciousness in different vessels. Your tutelary Deity sent you here." A devotee of Devi (goddess tradition) receives a vision of the Goddess during Swami Samartha's darshan — the two presences merge in her experience. The veeda (betel leaf) received from a Goddess temple appears in Swami Samartha's hand — the living demonstration that all divine forms are expressions of the single reality He embodies.
Section 17 ✦
God as Supreme Spirit
The philosophical sections of the biography — rarer than the miracle accounts but crucial. Swami Samartha's direct teachings on the nature of the divine reality: "I am not this body. I was never born and will never die. What you see before you is a convenience — a form the formless has adopted so you can receive what is already yours." The craftsman who sees Lord Shiva's form in Swami Samartha's body during darshan — a vision described in precise detail. Govindrao's confirming dream that night. Chintopant Tol's private conversation in which Swami Samartha speaks of the Brahman directly, without metaphor or parable. These sections establish that His miracles were not departures from natural law but demonstrations of a deeper natural law — the law of the Supreme Spirit.
Section 18 ✦
Showered With Kind Grace
Episodes of pure, unmotivated, spontaneous grace — given to devotees who had not asked for anything specific and sometimes to complete strangers. Jamanabai, a young woman in distress about an impossible marriage situation, is protected from the storm on her way to the temple; Swami Samartha arranges her marriage through a dream given to the right person. The silk dhoti that appears on a poor devotee's doorstep the morning of his daughter's wedding — Swami Samartha having been seen near the house the night before. Balaji Sadoba whose train is delayed by Swami Samartha so he arrives exactly in time to prevent a catastrophe. The train delay story — Swami Samartha physically stops a locomotive, documented by the British railway conductor.
Section 19 ✦
Master Do Not Forsake Us
The anguish of devotees when they fear losing access to Swami Samartha — through His apparent displeasure, His physical departure, or the approach of Mahasamadhi. Episodes in which devotees beg Him not to leave — and His response to each. Nrusimha Saraswati of Alandi (not to be confused with the earlier avatar) travels to Akkalkot out of deep longing and receives extended time with Swami Samartha. A Muslim saint from a Sufi lineage who arrives, is tested, and receives Swami Samartha's direct blessing — the transcendence of religious boundary treated as entirely natural. Baba Gholap of Nashik becomes a devoted follower after Swami Samartha appears in his dream and resolves an impossible situation. Each account ends with Swami Samartha's promise: "I will never abandon you."
Section 20 ✦
Measure of Splendour
The devotees who became instruments of Swami Samartha's grace — through whom His presence flowed into the world. Swamisut (Haribhau Bhivgade), who became a disciple by surrendering completely to Swami Samartha's direct command. Narasappa of Mysore, who walked barefoot from Karnataka to Akkalkot and received initiation. Vasudeo Balwant Phadke — the revolutionary freedom fighter — who visited Swami Samartha before his uprising; Swami Samartha gave him a cryptic instruction that Phadke interpreted as blessing. The disciples who carried the leelas beyond Akkalkot — translating them into Marathi texts, Telugu accounts, and oral traditions — became the channels of the parampara after Mahasamadhi.
Section 21 ✦
Primary Disciples
Detailed accounts of Swami Samartha's innermost circle of disciples: Cholappa — His constant companion and primary attendant, who was present at the Mahasamadhi; Sundarabai — the devoted woman who served Him with the simplicity of a child and was treated with extraordinary tenderness; Balappa — who managed the practical affairs of the ashram; Vamanbua Vamorikar — a wandering spiritual seeker who found his life's purpose in Swami Samartha's service; Nana Rekhi — the merchant devotee whose faith was absolute. Each is presented with their specific relationship dynamic with Swami Samartha, their particular leelas, and their spiritual development under His guidance.
Section 22 ✦
Royal Personalities
Swami Samartha's encounters with the rulers and nobles of 19th-century Maharashtra — a period of late Maratha power and emerging British authority. King Maloji Raje of Akkalkot — who was both skeptic and devotee, whose encounters with Swami Samartha form some of the most richly documented episodes in the biography. Maharaja Shinde of Gwalior makes the journey to Akkalkot. Tukojirao Holkar of Indore sends his emissaries and later comes personally. The story of Tatyasaheb and the empty palanquin — in which Swami Samartha sends a palanquin back to a noble's estate without entering it, yet the servants waiting at the other end swear they saw someone inside. The royal encounters reveal a Swami Samartha who is entirely comfortable with power — and entirely unimpressed by it.
Section 23 ✦
Swaroop Sampradaya
The establishment of the spiritual lineage that would carry Swami Samartha's presence forward after His physical departure — the Swaroop Sampradaya. The key disciples who received initiation and the authority to transmit: Ramanand Beedkar Maharaj; Deo Mamaledar; Srikrishna Saraswati (Kumbhar Swami); Vasudevanant Saraswati (Baba Maharaj Sahasrabuddhe, whose samadhi mandir is now at Chatushrungi Road, Pune); Digambardas Maharaj. Each received a specific form of Swami Samartha's presence — a different aspect of the divine reality — and each carried that aspect into their own teaching. The Swaroop Sampradaya is Swami Samartha's answer to the question "What happens after Mahasamadhi?" — the answer being: "The Guru's presence distributes through the parampara rather than concentrating in a single body."
Section 24 ✦
Temple Information
The principal temple and samadhi shrines associated with Swami Samartha and His lineage. The primary Akkalkot samadhi mandir — still the central pilgrimage site in Maharashtra for the Swami Samartha tradition, drawing hundreds of thousands of devotees annually. Shree Sadguru Baba Maharaj Sahasrabuddhe Samadhi Mandir, 937/D Chatushrungi Road, Shivajinagar, Pune 411016 — the samadhi of Swami Samartha's principal disciple Vasudevanant Saraswati, still active with daily rituals and parayan. Akkalkot-Niwasi Shree Swami Samarth Mandir, Off Four Bunglows, Andheri West, Mumbai 400053 — serving the large Swami Samartha devotee community in Mumbai. Shree Shiv-Samarth Gad, Shree Kshetra Dervan, Chiplun, Ratnagiri — in the Konkan coast, associated with the Swaroop Sampradaya tradition.
Key Persons — Shri Swami Samartha Akkalkot
15+ persons
🔱
Swami Samartha of Akkalkot
Divine Subject — 3rd Datta Avatar
The third incarnation of Lord Dattatreya in Kali Yuga. Appeared at Akkalkot (Prajnapura), Sholapur district, Maharashtra, in Shake 1779 (1856 CE), on Ashwin Shuddha Panchami. Directly identified Himself as Narasimha Saraswati. Present in Akkalkot for 21 years; Mahasamadhi on Chaitra Shuddha Trayodashi, Shake 1800 (1878 CE). Came from the Kadali Van — the forest that had received Narasimha Saraswati four centuries earlier. His biography is a documentation of uninterrupted divine presence across the centuries of the Datta Parampara.
📿
Cholappa
Primary Disciple — Constant Companion
The closest and most constant of Swami Samartha's disciples. Cholappa was present throughout the Akkalkot years and was at Swami Samartha's side at the Mahasamadhi. He managed Swami Samartha's daily needs with complete surrender — feeding Him, attending to His physical comfort, and acting as the first point of contact for the thousands of devotees who came seeking darshan. His role in the biography is that of the ideal attendant-disciple: ego-less, present, effective.
🌸
Sundarabai
Devoted Disciple — Served with Child-Like Simplicity
The devoted woman who served Swami Samartha with the purity and directness of a child. She knew no Sanskrit, no philosophy, no elaborate ritual — only a total, unself-conscious love. Swami Samartha treated her with extraordinary tenderness that He rarely showed to learned or prominent devotees. Her example is used throughout the biography to illustrate: simple, sincere bhakti without intellectual sophistication is the most direct route to the Guru's grace.
🏛️
Balappa
Disciple — Ashram Manager
The practical backbone of Swami Samartha's Akkalkot residence. Balappa managed the considerable logistics of running what was effectively a spiritual center receiving thousands of visitors — food, shelter, discipline, the rotation of access to Swami Samartha's presence. After the Mahasamadhi, Swami Samartha appeared to Balappa directly to comfort him and confirm that the presence had not ended with the body. The biography records his grief and his consolation as a final gift from the departed Guru.
🧘
Vamanbua Vamorikar
Disciple — Wandering Seeker Settled
A wandering spiritual seeker from a family of devout Vaishnavas who found his permanent spiritual home in Swami Samartha's presence. His initiation at Akkalkot brought an end to years of restless seeking across pilgrimage sites and teachers. His story is a classic pattern in the Datta tradition: the soul that searches everywhere finally finds the Guru, and in the finding, recognises that everything it was searching for was always here. He became one of the transmitters of the parampara tradition after Mahasamadhi.
🙏
Swamisut (Haribhau Bhivgade)
Disciple — Surrendered by Direct Command
Known in the parampara as Swamisut ("Son of Swami"), Haribhau Bhivgade became a disciple through Swami Samartha's direct command rather than through his own decision. Swami Samartha addressed him by name — a name He had not been told — and said: "You are mine now." Haribhau's total, immediate surrender is the biography's model of sharan (complete refuge): the Guru claims the disciple before the disciple has fully chosen. He later became a significant teacher in his own right, carrying the Swaroop Sampradaya forward.
💼
Nana Rekhi
Devotee — Merchant of Absolute Faith
A successful merchant from the region around Akkalkot whose faith in Swami Samartha was absolute and without conditions. He made no requests, imposed no expectations, and placed his entire business under Swami Samartha's protection — not as a strategy but as a genuine surrender. The biography records his prospering business as a natural consequence of this surrender, and several of the section-14 and section-15 miracles occur in or around his household. He became a significant donor and supporter of the parampara tradition.
🔱
Ramanand Beedkar Maharaj
Disciple — Swaroop Sampradaya
One of the significant disciples through whom Swami Samartha's spiritual presence was transmitted after Mahasamadhi. Ramanand Beedkar Maharaj received initiation and carried the parampara into his own teaching. His lineage became one of the main branches of the Swaroop Sampradaya — the system of transmission that Swami Samartha established specifically so that the divine presence would continue through qualified disciples rather than dying with the physical body.
👑
Maloji Raje
Royal Devotee — King of Akkalkot
The ruling king of Akkalkot during the period of Swami Samartha's residence. His relationship with Swami Samartha was complex — oscillating between sincere devotion and administrative caution about a powerful saint in his territory. He receives the blessing of an heir through Swami Samartha's direct intervention. His palace is effectively sanctified by Swami Samartha's presence, and several key episodes occur in the royal compound. After his initial ambivalence, Maloji Raje became one of Swami Samartha's most devoted protectors.
⚔️
Vasudeo Balwant Phadke
Historical — Revolutionary Freedom Fighter
The legendary 19th-century Indian revolutionary — sometimes called the "Father of the Armed Uprising against British Rule in India" — who visited Swami Samartha before launching his uprising. Swami Samartha gave him a cryptic instruction that Phadke interpreted as a blessing for his mission. The historical intersection of the Datta tradition with the Indian independence movement — through one of its most dramatic figures — makes this episode one of the most cited in the biography.
⚖️
Chintopant Tol
Devotee — Saved from Legal Ruin
A government official threatened with serious legal consequences through false accusation. His faith in Swami Samartha was absolute; he placed the entire matter in Swami Samartha's hands and refused to engage a lawyer. The case was dismissed through what observers described as a sequence of improbable coincidences — each of which Swami Samartha had, in private conversation with Chintopant, already described before it happened. His story became one of the definitive examples of Swami Samartha as divine advocate in legal and official matters.
🛕
Vasudevanant Saraswati (Baba Maharaj Sahasrabuddhe)
Principal Disciple — Swaroop Sampradaya
One of Swami Samartha's most significant disciples and a principal vehicle of the Swaroop Sampradaya. His samadhi mandir — Shree Sadguru Baba Maharaj Sahasrabuddhe Samadhi Mandir — is at 937/D Chatushrungi Road, Shivajinagar, Pune 411016, and remains an active pilgrimage centre. He carried both Swami Samartha's direct transmission and an elaborate ritual and philosophical structure that made the parampara accessible to householder devotees across Maharashtra and beyond.
🏡
Nrusimhabhan
The First Host — Yajurvedi Brahmin
The devout Yajurvedi Brahmin of Kashyap Gotra who first recognised and sheltered Swami Samartha on His arrival at Akkalkot. His instinctive recognition of the divine in the wandering stranger — before anyone else had formed an opinion — places him in the honoured position of "first devotee." His household was the first home of Swami Samartha's Akkalkot presence, and his Kashyap lineage connection with the Datta tradition (Dattatreya's father Atri being of the Kashyap lineage) is considered cosmically significant in the parampara tradition.
🔱
Digambardas Maharaj
Disciple — Swaroop Sampradaya Transmitter
One of the recognised disciples of Swami Samartha who carried the parampara forward after Mahasamadhi. His initiation was direct — Swami Samartha personally acknowledged him as a vehicle of transmission. The Swaroop Sampradaya functions through a network of such initiated teachers, each carrying a specific aspect of Swami Samartha's presence, so that no single successor can claim to be the "complete" continuation — the total presence is distributed across the parampara rather than concentrated in one body, exactly as Swami Samartha designed it.
🏺
Srikrishna Saraswati (Kumbhar Swami)
Disciple — Cross-Caste Transmission
Known as Kumbhar Swami — "the Swami from the Potter caste" — his inclusion in the innermost circle of Swami Samartha's initiated disciples is a deliberate statement about the caste-transcendence of the Datta tradition. A member of the Kumbhar (potter) community who received direct initiation from Swami Samartha, became a sannyasi, and taught as Srikrishna Saraswati. His story was cited by Maharashtra's social reformers — B.R. Ambedkar's generation knew it well — as evidence that the living Datta tradition, at its best, had always rejected caste hierarchy.
Sacred Places — Shri Swami Samartha Akkalkot
16+ places
⭐
Akkalkot (Prajnapura)
Primary Seat — Sholapur, Maharashtra
The city of Swami Samartha's 21-year residence. Ancient name: Prajnapura (City of Wisdom). Located in Sholapur district, Maharashtra. The main Swami Samartha Samadhi Mandir is here — still the most visited pilgrimage site associated with this tradition. The city's name literally means "the place of Akkal (wisdom/discernment)" — the same quality that is Swami Samartha's essential gift to seekers.
📖 Wikipedia ↗🗺 Maps ↗
📍
Mangalvedha
Town — Solapur, Maharashtra
An important town adjacent to Akkalkot in Solapur district. Associated with devotee accounts and several leelas that occur in the region between Mangalvedha and Akkalkot. Historically significant as the birth place of Sant Chokhamela — the Dalit saint whose memory, like Swami Samartha's caste-transcendent grace, challenged the rigid hierarchies of Brahminic Maharashtra.
📖 Wikipedia ↗🗺 Maps ↗
📍
Mohol
Town — Sholapur, Maharashtra
A town in Sholapur district connected to Akkalkot through the network of Swami Samartha's devotees. Several leelas recorded in the biography involve devotees from Mohol who make the journey to Akkalkot. Part of the Sholapur region that formed the core geography of Swami Samartha's earthly manifestation.
🗺 Maps ↗
⭐
Pandharpur
Great Pilgrimage City — Maharashtra
The great Vaishnava pilgrimage city on the Bhima river; seat of Vitthala (Pandurang). Associated with Swami Samartha's connections to devotees of the Varkari (Pandharpur pilgrimage) tradition. Several devotees in the biography are Varkaris who first visit Akkalkot after Pandharpur — and Swami Samartha's ease with Vaishnava devotees reflects the Datta tradition's consistent embrace of all Hindu paths as expressions of the single divine reality.
📖 Wikipedia ↗🗺 Maps ↗
⭐
Ganagapura (Gangapur)
Sacred Seat of Previous Avatar — Karnataka
The great seat of Narasimha Saraswati (the second Datta avatar) on the Bhima-Amaraja confluence in Gulbarga district, Karnataka. Referenced throughout the Swami Samartha biography as the precedent and prefiguration of Akkalkot — the two seats of two avatars forming a geographical parampara across the Deccan. Swami Samartha Himself identifies with Narasimha Saraswati, and the Ganagapura connection is fundamental to understanding the continuous single identity beneath the two forms.
📖 Wikipedia ↗🗺 Maps ↗
🏔️
Girnar (Junagadh)
Sacred Mountain — Gujarat
The great sacred mountain of the Datta tradition in Junagadh district, Gujarat. The Dattatreya temple at Girnar's peak (accessed by the famous 10,000-step climb) is one of the most sacred Datta pilgrimage sites in India. Swami Samartha is associated with Girnar in biographical traditions — His apparent journey from the South, through the Datta sacred sites of western India, before arriving at Akkalkot traces the living geography of the Dattatreya parampara.
📖 Wikipedia ↗🗺 Maps ↗
🛕
Jagannath Puri
Pilgrimage — Odisha
The great coastal pilgrimage city in Odisha — one of the four Char Dhams of India. Associated with Swami Samartha through biographical accounts of His all-India wandering before Akkalkot. The Datta tradition has deep connections with the Jagannath tradition — both emphasise universal divine grace beyond caste and ritual purity. Some accounts in the biography reference Swami Samartha's presence at Puri before His final northward journey toward Akkalkot.
📖 Wikipedia ↗🗺 Maps ↗
🌴
Kadali Van (Plantain Forest)
Sacred Forest — Origin of Swami Samartha
The same sacred plantain forest near Srisailam (Nallamala Hills, Andhra Pradesh) into which Narasimha Saraswati disappeared in 1459 CE. Swami Samartha's biography places His origin in this forest — establishing the unbroken parampara connection. The Kadali Van is thus the meeting point of two avatars in time: the second entering in 1459 CE, the third emerging (in the biography's theological reading) four centuries later to appear at Akkalkot. A place where the visible and invisible continuity of the Datta line become one.
🗺 Maps ↗
🏞️
Nasik (Nashik)
Pilgrimage City — Maharashtra
Ancient sacred city on the Godavari river; one of the four Kumbh Mela sites. Nrusimha Saraswati of Alandi (not the second avatar) makes his journey to Akkalkot from this region. Baba Gholap of Nashik is a biographical subject. The Nashik region — historically the zone of multiple overlapping saint traditions in Maharashtra — connects to Swami Samartha's biography through several devotees who come from Nashik and the Godavari belt.
📖 Wikipedia ↗🗺 Maps ↗
🛕
Idagi / Jehur
Village Temples — Karnataka / Maharashtra
Two locations referenced in the biography's miracle accounts. At Jehur, rain falls during a severe drought after villagers pray through Swami Samartha's name. At Idagi (Karnataka), Vaishnava villages threatened with collective legal punishment receive liberation through Swami Samartha's intervention from Akkalkot — hundreds of miles away. Both locations illustrate the geographical reach of His grace: physical distance from Akkalkot imposes no limits on the protection available to sincere devotees.
🗺 Maps ↗
🌊
Rameshwar / Kotikoop
Pilgrimage Site — Rameshwaram
The great southern pilgrimage city of Rameshwaram — one of the four Char Dhams — and the adjacent Kotikoop sacred tank. Swami Samartha circumambulates a Rameshwar temple, and the locked doors open by themselves in His presence. The Kotikoop miracle is one of the most cited in the biography: a local priest who refused Him entry watches the temple acknowledge what he had denied. The Rameshwaram episode establishes that Swami Samartha's all-India wandering before Akkalkot covered both the northern and southern sacred circuits.
📖 Wikipedia ↗🗺 Maps ↗
🛕
Pune (Shivajinagar)
Swaroop Sampradaya Centre — Maharashtra
Home of the Shree Sadguru Baba Maharaj Sahasrabuddhe Samadhi Mandir at 937/D Chatushrungi Road, Shivajinagar, Pune 411016 — the samadhi of Swami Samartha's principal disciple Vasudevanant Saraswati. The Pune centre is one of the main living nodes of the Swaroop Sampradaya, with daily rituals, annual parayan events, and a substantial library of Datta and Swami Samartha literature.

🏙️
Mumbai (Andheri West)
Temple — Mumbai, Maharashtra
Home of the Akkalkot-Niwasi Shree Swami Samarth Mandir, Off Four Bunglows, Andheri West, Mumbai 400053. Serves the large and historically significant Swami Samartha devotee community in Mumbai. The Kandewadi locality (now absorbed into the city) was an early centre of Swami Samartha devotion in Bombay during the late 19th and early 20th century, through the first-generation disciples who had personally received His darshan.
🗺 Maps ↗
📍
Baroda (Vadodara)
City — Gujarat
The royal city of Baroda in Gujarat, connected to the biography through the account of royal devotees from the Gaekwad dynasty who made the journey to Akkalkot to seek Swami Samartha's darshan. The royal patronage from Gujarat established early connections between the Swami Samartha tradition and the Gujarati business and noble communities — connections that persist in the form of significant Swami Samartha centres in Gujarat today.
📖 Wikipedia ↗🗺 Maps ↗
🌸
Kolhapur
Sacred City — Maharashtra
The city of Mahalakshmi in southern Maharashtra. Connected to Swami Samartha's biography through the Datta tradition's historical presence in the Kolhapur region and the accounts of devotees from this area who made the pilgrimage to Akkalkot. The Kolhapur region is part of the dense sacred geography connecting Akkalkot, Pandharpur, Narsoba Wadi, and the Datta sacred sites of southern Maharashtra and northern Karnataka.
📖 Wikipedia ↗🗺 Maps ↗
🏔️
Dervan, Ratnagiri (Chiplun)
Swaroop Sampradaya Site — Konkan Coast
Home of Shree Shiv-Samarth Gad — an important Swaroop Sampradaya temple site on the Konkan coast near Chiplun, Ratnagiri district. The Konkan coast's deep Datta devotion tradition — historically the homeground of many Brahmin families who became Swami Samartha devotees in the 19th century — is represented here. The Shiv-Samarth Gad continues as an active pilgrimage site within the Swaroop Sampradaya network.
